## 2. EmPOWER Legislation Overview and Impacts

### 2.1 Summary of Legislation

After adopting the Climate Solutions Now Act (CSNA) in 2022, which established
statewide decarbonization goals, the Maryland General Assembly adopted HB 864 in
2024 to articulate the EmPOWER program’s role in support of the CSNA. HB 864
reoriented EmPOWER by replacing the historic energy-savings framework with a GHG
reduction framework, expanding eligible program objectives to include beneficial
electrification, establishing GHG reduction targets, and articulating the role for both
behind-the-meter and front-of-the-meter measures, requiring that most reductions come
from behind-the-meter measures.
On May 12, 2026, Governor Wes Moore signed the Utility RELIEF Act into law. This bill
has significant impacts on the EmPOWER program including reducing program goals
from 2.5% to 1.75% of a 2016 baseline, removing gas utilities from the program, allowing
utilities to fulfill up to 20% of their obligation with solar, and instructing the Commission to
release a Request for Information on third-party program implementation. Together, HB
864 and the Utility RELIEF Act transformed EmPOWER from a traditional energy
efficiency program into a GHG reduction program, balancing climate objectives with
customer affordability.

### 2.2 Impact of Legislation on EmPOWER Goals

In legislation, both in HB 864 and the Utility RELIEF Act, EmPOWER’s lifecycle GHG
reduction goals are a translation of annual energy savings goals based on a percentage
of 2016 retail electricity sales. The translation from the clearly defined annual energy
savings to lifecycle GHG reduction requires two critical inputs to develop final goals: a
conversion from energy savings to GHG reduction and a conversion from annual savings
to lifecycle savings.
To convert electric energy savings to GHG reduction, the Utility RELIEF Act specifies the
use of the most recent emissions projections from the Maryland Department of the
Environment (MDE). Future Programming Work Group (FPWG) members developed a
methodology for estimating marginal emissions rates using current data from the U.S.
Department of Energy and PJM. This methodology was accepted by the group and
adopted by MDE as their most recent emissions projection. The new marginal emissions
rates used in developing the electric utility lifecycle GHG reduction goals and this resulting
plan for 2027–2029 vary significantly from the current emissions rates used in the

[PDF p. 24 | printed p. 14]

EmPOWER programs in 2025–2026. This change in emissions rates creates difficulty in
any direct comparison between the reported emissions reductions in 2025–2026
(Cycle 6) and the goals and projections for 2027–2029 (Cycle 7).
The second critical input to develop final lifecycle GHG reduction goals, translating annual
savings to lifecycle savings, is not clearly defined in legislation and is highly sensitive to
determinations made outside of the legislation. The annual-to-lifecycle conversion is a
complex matter as it considers the lifetime of energy savings for each measure offered
within EmPOWER portfolios. This issue became the most impactful non-consensus item
in the FPWG meetings held after the passage of the Utility RELIEF Act, with outcomes
described below.

### 2.3 Future Programming Work Group Outcomes

BGE acknowledges the substantial effort undertaken by the Future Programming Work
Group (FPWG) to develop a framework for translating the EmPOWER Maryland statutory
energy savings requirements into lifecycle GHG reduction goals for the 2027–2029
program cycle. The FPWG's original recommendation, as reflected in its October 2025
Status Report, established a consensus methodology utilizing the Time-adjusted
Reduction of Avoided Carbon Emissions (TRACE) framework, statewide weighted-
average EUL bins, and marginal emission rates to convert the statutory savings target
into lifecycle GHG reduction goals. The methodology was subsequently approved by the
Commission and served as the foundation for future goal-setting discussions.
Following enactment of the Utility RELIEF Act, the FPWG reconvened to evaluate the
impact of the revised statutory requirements, including the reduction of the electric
savings target from 2.5% to 1.75% of 2016 weather-normalized retail sales, removal of
gas utility goals, and authorization for solar resources to contribute up to 20% of goal
achievement. While FPWG participants generally agreed on incorporating the revised
statutory targets and updated MDE marginal emission rates, significant differences
emerged regarding how the revised goals should be translated into lifecycle GHG
reduction targets.
Within the FPWG, BGE, Pepco, Delmarva Power, Potomac Edison, and the Maryland
Energy Administration (MEA) supported an approach grounded in demonstrated 2025
program performance and an electric statewide weighted-average EUL of approximately
seven years. In contrast, PSC Staff, MDE, the Office of People’s Council (OPC), MEEA,
Montgomery County, and other stakeholders advocated for continuing the previously
approved EUL bin methodology, while SMECO proposed a modified version of the bin
approach scaled to a seven-year EUL. These differing perspectives were documented in

[PDF p. 25 | printed p. 15]

the FPWG's May 2026 report and subsequently reviewed by the Commission in
Order No. 92522.
A central issue identified by both the FPWG and Order No. 92522 is the appropriate EUL
assumption to use when translating statutory savings requirements into lifecycle GHG
reduction goals. The FPWG's originally recommended methodology produced an
effective portfolio EUL of approximately 9.6 years after excluding conservation voltage
reduction (CVR) and combined heat and power (CHP) from the goal-setting framework
and emphasizing longer-lived measures through the EUL bin structure.
BGE's position, as presented through the FPWG process, is that a seven-year EUL more
accurately reflects actual statewide portfolio performance observed during 2025, the first-
year utilities operated under lifecycle GHG reduction goals. The utility-supported
methodology relies upon demonstrated program results rather than forecasted portfolio
assumptions and therefore provides a realistic and transparent basis for establishing
future goals. The FPWG's analysis showed that the electric EmPOWER utilities achieved
a statewide weighted-average EUL of approximately seven years during 2025, which
BGE believes is a more representative measure of current market performance and
customer adoption patterns. BGE believes that a seven-year EUL appropriately balances
Maryland's GHG reduction objectives with affordability considerations while remaining
grounded in actual portfolio performance.
The FPWG devoted considerable attention to the role of DHCD in achieving Maryland's
future EmPOWER GHG reduction targets. Throughout the FPWG process, stakeholders
identified substantial uncertainty regarding both the interpretation of DHCD's statutory
obligations and the way DHCD savings should be incorporated into utility goal
calculations. These issues remain among the most significant factors affecting future goal
development and budget planning.
The FPWG's October 2025 Status Report provided a consensus recommendation that
utility and DHCD goals be separated so that each entity remains accountable for only its
own performance. FPWG participants generally agreed that while DHCD savings
contribute toward statewide GHG reductions, utilities should not be held responsible for
achieving savings generated by a separate organization over which they have no
operational control. The FPWG also recognized that the allocation of DHCD savings
affects the magnitude of utility-specific goals and therefore has important implications for
future program design and spending requirements.
Subsequent FPWG discussions in 2026 highlighted the potential scale of DHCD's
contribution to statewide GHG reductions. DHCD presented projections showing

[PDF p. 26 | printed p. 16]

materially higher levels of savings than its statutory minimum requirements and indicated
that these achievements may be attainable with budgets comparable to current funding
levels. However, questions remain regarding the appropriate level of DHCD performance,
how those savings should be credited toward statewide objectives, and whether DHCD
savings should reduce utility obligations or be evaluated independently.
From BGE's perspective, the FPWG record supports maintaining clear lines of
accountability for both DHCD-administered programs and utility-administered
EmPOWER programs. Establishing separate goals for DHCD and the utilities promotes
transparency, facilitates accurate performance evaluation, and avoids assigning utilities
responsibility for savings outside of their control. While DHCD has historically claimed
savings produced by the EmPOWER utilities, the recent FPWG recommendation to set
each party an independent GHG emissions reduction goal, changes this precedent for
Cycle 7. On August 20, 2026, the Commission issued an Order on Request for
Determination which accepted this recommendation by the FPWG.[^p26n8] There are still some
questions around how the DHCD savings and utility savings will interact moving forward,
but the Commission's adoption of this recommendation ensures that statewide GHG
reduction goals are distributed in a manner that is equitable, achievable, and consistent
with statutory requirements.

### 2.4 Alignment of Goals With MDE Plan

Commission Order No. 92522 notes that the Utility RELIEF Act “requires that, when
establishing a goal, the Commission must consider the most recent MDE plan to meet
the State’s climate goals.”[^p26n9] As such, the order requires that the utilities “explain explicitly
how the 7-year EUL is in alignment with the plan versus the EUL of 9.6.”[^p26n10] The EmPOWER
goal-setting methodology contains four basic elements controlled by three
different parties:

**Figure 2: EmPOWER Goal-Setting Methodology**

[DIAGRAM DESCRIPTION: Figure 2: EmPOWER Goal-Setting Methodology]

An equation diagram with four boxed terms joined by multiplication signs: (2016 Wholesale MWh) × (Goal Percentage) in an orange box labeled "State"; × (Lifetime Portfolio Conversion) in a purple box labeled "PSC"; × (GHG Emission Intensity) in a green box labeled "MDE"; then × 3 Years = Goal. In other words, Goal = (2016 Wholesale MWh × Goal Percentage) × Lifetime Portfolio Conversion × GHG Emission Intensity × 3 Years, with the first two terms controlled by the State, the third by the PSC (Commission) and the fourth by the Maryland Department of the Environment (MDE).

[→ See original PDF page 26 for visual rendering]

[^p26n8]: ORDER ON REQUEST FOR DETERMINATION, August 20, 2026 – ML333462

[^p26n9]: ORDER NO. 92522, July 10, 2026 – ML 332009

[^p26n10]: ORDER NO. 92522, July 10, 2026 – ML 332009

[PDF p. 27 | printed p. 17]

The baseline (2016 electric sales) and energy savings goal percentage (1.75%) were
both set in the Utility RELIEF Act by the state legislature. Alignment with the MDE plan
comes from the emissions intensity calculations, which HB 864 required come from
MDE’s most recent data and projections. The final portion of the equation, translation of
the goal from annual to lifecycle using an EUL estimate, was left up to the Commission in
the Utility RELIEF Act. Because the same MDE emissions intensities were used when
calculating a goal using EULs of seven and 9.6, both goal-setting methodologies are
equally in alignment with the MDE plan.

## 3. Portfolio Analysis and Program Design

### 3.1 Portfolio Objectives and Themes

When BGE set out to design and model the High and Low Scenarios, BGE kept customer
affordability, customer experience, and compliance with the Utility RELIEF Act at the
forefront. BGE sought to design two implementable portfolios that balance measures and
programs that produce reliable lifecycle GHG savings with innovative new delivery
approaches. The designed portfolios confidently and assuredly achieve the
corresponding lifecycle GHG emission reduction goals in an affordable, reliable, and
effective manner. The proposed programs will encourage participation across all
customer groups; deliver a seamless, customer-centered experience; and reinforce
BGE’s reputation as a leader in innovative demand-side management programs while
providing measurable benefits to the grid, our customers, and to all Marylanders.
While planning the High and Low Scenarios, some key themes emerged in both. These
include optimizing budgets, shifting investment to residential electrification measures,
which have a very low cost per ton of GHG saved, and significantly reducing low-cost
commercial lighting opportunities due to the reduction in their GHG savings. Both
scenarios included close review of budgets for all programs, slimming existing budgets
wherever possible and reorganizing programs to run more cost-efficiently. The many
external changes to the upcoming cycle — including those driven by updates to the
Maryland Technical Reference Manual (TRM), evaluation feedback, and GHG emissions
factors — have collectively reduced many of the low-cost savings opportunities in the
non-residential sector. The external factors influencing this portfolio design are discussed
in more detail in section 3.3, but broadly the significant reductions in commercial lighting
savings paired with a major increase in residential electrification investment has led to the
total EmPOWER budget shifting more to the residential sector.

[PDF p. 28 | printed p. 18]

### 3.2 Stakeholder Outreach and Coordination

#### 3.2.1 Stakeholder Engagement Overview

Stakeholder engagement is a critical component of the EmPOWER cycle planning
process. Without the valuable input we receive from parties with viewpoints other than
our own, we could not develop or implement programs that rise to the standards that BGE
holds itself to. BGE developed a stakeholder strategy as one of the first activities in the
planning process to ensure that we would have enough time to not only engage these
parties near the end once the plan was solidified, but build relationships, have multiple
conversations, and truly listen to their ideas and feedback. BGE’s approach to
engagement was grounded in ensuring that we talked to a wide range of stakeholders,
not only the usual intervenors, but also parties who might feel removed from what can
often be an extremely technical process.
In kicking off BGE’s planning process for this filing, we started the stakeholder
engagement process early at the beginning of the year. We sought input from a broad
range of stakeholders, many of whom we have engaged continually in the past as well as
newer stakeholders. The BGE team sought feedback from stakeholders on current
offerings, potential new offerings, and other opportunities for the BGE EmPOWER
Portfolio in Cycle 7. We endeavored to listen to every perspective and hear candid
feedback. We met with advocacy groups, community organizations, contractors and trade
groups, energy efficiency industry groups, environmental advocates, as well as State and
County Government agencies and departments.
The broad range of stakeholders that BGE met with included The American Council for
an Energy-Efficient Economy (ACEEE), Baltimore City Office of Sustainability, Center for
Progressive Reform, Evergreen Action, Green & Healthy Homes Initiative (GHHI),
Howard County Office of Community Sustainability, Maryland Building Industry
Association (MBIA), Maryland Department of Housing and Community Development
(DHCD), Maryland Department of the Environment (MDE), Maryland Energy
Administration (MEA), Maryland Energy Advocates Coalition, Maryland Energy Efficiency
Advocates’ (MEEA), Maryland Heating and Air Conditioning Contractors, Maryland PIRG,
Montogomery County Department of Environmental Protection, National Consumer Law
Center, Public Service Commission Staff, the Office of Peoples Council (OPC), Rocky
Mountain Institute (RMI), and The Sierra Club. The outreach engagements included over
27 meetings with these stakeholder groups.

[PDF p. 29 | printed p. 19]

Some key themes and feedback from the stakeholder meetings included:

- Acknowledgement and appreciation for the success of the BGE programs and a
  strong desire for the programs to continue.

- A desire to add incentives for window heat pumps.
- Reinforcement of the importance of ensuring EmPOWER programs serve and
  reach more low to moderate income customers.

- Emphasis on the importance of providing a better customer experience to meet
  customers where they are.

- Contractors expressed support for EmPOWER, but shared concerns around
  reduced goals under the Utility RELIEF Act and reduced budgets for the
  programs that they were involved in such as HVAC Midstream and Home
  Performance.

- Lack of clear understanding of some program elements and offerings that were
  part of BGE’s current EmPOWER Portfolio.

- Suggestions on opportunities to further align BGE’s EmPOWER offerings with
  other external initiatives such as non-profit grants that could further expand the
  reach of benefits to customers.

- Acknowledgement of the quality work BGE has done to partner with DHCD, and
  an expressed a desire for more work to be done to help customers most in need.

- Appreciation of BGE’s Moderate Income Offer, but a desire to serve more
  moderate income customers and pursue greater promotion of the offer.
  Through the stakeholder engagement process it became clear that stakeholders aren’t
  necessarily looking for expanded measures or new technologies to be included in the
  portfolio, rather they are looking for increased and improved access, ease of use, and
  more education around the programs and measures offered.

#### 3.2.2 Influence on Portfolio Development

The BGE team sought to incorporate the stakeholder feedback received during outreach
meetings into our portfolio development wherever feasible. This included:

- Expanding the moderate income offering to increase participation. BGE
  acknowledges the initial offer that put forth in Cycle 6 was purposefully launched
  in a more conservative way to be able to ensure very close coordination with
  DHCD and test the design of the offer.

- Developing a new approach in collaboration with DHCD, called the Multifamily
  Customer Navigation Service, to help our renter population and multifamily
  building owners access BGE EmPOWER and DHCD EmPOWER offerings more
  seamlessly and direct customers to the appropriate program based on eligibility.

[PDF p. 30 | printed p. 20]

- Continuing and building on a strong partnership with DHCD, which includes
  increasing the coordination efforts to support DHCD in lead generation and co-
  branding marketing materials and joint outreach opportunities.

- Improving how customers access and navigate BGE’s residential and non-
  residential programs, through more streamlined, program entry points that
  reduce barriers to participation.

- Including window heat pumps in the program measure list.
- Collaborating with stakeholders on initiatives outside of EmPOWER, including
  but not limited to initiatives by State Agencies, Counties, as well as local non-
  profit organizations. This could include:

  - Further coordination with MDE on the Maryland Building Energy
    Performance Standards (BEPS) and connecting customers subject to
    BEPS with BGE’s reporting tools and programs.

  - Work with Maryland Energy Administration (MEA) on their Residential
    Energy Equity Heat Pump Rebate Program.

  - Engaging with Counties on current and planned initiatives which could
    expand the reach and awareness of BGE’s EmPOWER Programs, an
    example being the Baltimore City Energy Challenge initiative.

  - Engagement with non-profits on initiatives that could combine EmPOWER
    incentives with grants funding to enhance benefits to customers.
    The BGE team looks forward to continuing ongoing stakeholder engagement, after the
    Plan is filed and throughout Cycle 7. To continue delivering leading programs to our
    customers we need to continue to engage with a broad range of stakeholders. This
    includes seeking feedback on existing programs as well as collaboration on new ideas
    and initiatives.

#### 3.2.3 Coordination With DHCD

BGE remains committed to our strong ongoing coordination with DHCD and achieving
positive outcomes for low- and moderate-income residents and historically underserved
and underrepresented communities. BGE and DHCD meet on a regular basis to
coordinate planning efforts and find areas of alignment to overcome challenges facing
limited-income customers. These activities include:

- BGE identifying and sending customer referrals to DHCD.
- Sending lead lists on a regular cadence to DHCD of limited-income customers
  with high energy use and reconnects.

- Inclusion of educational materials about DHCD programs in outreach and
  marketing.

- Joint attendance at community events.

[PDF p. 31 | printed p. 21]

- Joint trainings for contractors and trade partners.
- Coordination with DHCD in designing and implementing program-specific offers
  and special promotions, such as no-cost smart thermostats.

- Joint content creation for marketing campaigns targeted to limited-income
  customers.

- Bill messages and insert space in utility bills to drive awareness and participation
  in DHCD programs.
  BGE includes DHCD in discussions that involve the EmPOWER programs and activities
  that benefit limited-income customers to ensure consistent messaging and alignment of
  efforts. BGE is committed to continuing these activities with DHCD and maintaining a
  strong, healthy channel of communication to ensure sustained partnership and constant
  improvement.
  BGE is proposing to continue its coordinated efforts in the upcoming cycle, building off
  the success of Cycle 6’s coordinated efforts. BGE is proposing that utility administrative
  and utility marketing budgets be used again in Cycle 7 for enhanced coordination with
  DHCD. The utility administrative bucket will allow BGE to allocate more resources to
  ensure DHCD gets the dedicated resources that they need from us. The proposed DHCD
  Marketing budget will be for coordinated marketing between BGE and DHCD to support
  awareness and promotion of DHCD’s EmPOWER programs. In Cycle 7, the coordination
  budget will also be used to support the Multifamily Customer Navigation Service
  described in section 3.3.3.

### 3.3 Portfolio Development Process

Under Commission Order No. 92522 on EmPOWER Semi-Annual and Work Group
Reports, utilities are directed to file two separate scenarios designed to achieve the GHG
emissions reduction goals that result from utilizing different EULs in the goal-setting
calculations.

#### 3.3.1 Existing Portfolio Analysis and Key Updates

BGE analyzed the 2025–2026 EmPOWER MD Program Filing Additional Plan
Information Case No. 9705 (ML 312026) in conjunction with actual performance data from
the 2025 EmPOWER program year as a basis for development of the 2027–2029
program plan. The 2025–2026 Additional Program Filing was the first EmPOWER filing
to incorporate a specific GHG reduction goal for each utility. As such, these recent
portfolio designs and results provided a better starting point for planning the 2027–2029
EmPOWER portfolio than other historic cycle plans.

[PDF p. 32 | printed p. 22]

The FPWG and Evaluation Advisory Group (EAG) provided updated GHG emissions
values for the 2027–2029 cycle that differ from those used from 2025–2026. The updated
emissions factors used marginal GHG emissions instead of average annual GHG
emissions. Marginal GHG emissions are calculated using the estimated emissions from
marginal generation that increases or decreases to meet a change in demand during peak
energy usage periods. The marginal generation brought online during peak periods often
has the worst GHG emissions profiles for electrical generation.
To create a baseline of existing programs and measure offerings, BGE applied the
updated marginal GHG emissions factors to the 2025 historical portfolio performance data
to provide an equivalent comparison of current program performance applied to potential
2027–2029 performance. Figure 3 shows the 2025 portfolio performance when translating
reported energy savings to the updated GHG emissions values used for the 2027-2029
cycle in comparison to the proposed Plan scenarios. The 2025 GHG Reduction utilizes
the energy savings as claimed in 2025 while the proposed Plan Scenarios further reflect
portfolio changes made as well as realizing the evaluation impacts to measure savings.
Most significantly, the reduction in the non-residential sub-portfolio savings is attributable
to the reduction in claimable non-residential lighting savings.
**Figure 3: GHG Reduction Comparison of 2025 Reported Savings versus average 2027-2029 Plan Scenarios**

[DIAGRAM DESCRIPTION: Figure 3: GHG Reduction Comparison of 2025 Reported Savings versus average 2027-2029 Plan Scenarios]

A stacked bar chart titled "BGE Yearly Lifetime GHG Savings Comparison (MT GHG): 2025 Achievement vs. Avg Annual High/Low Scenario". Three bars on the x-axis: "2025 GHG Reduction", "2027-2029 High Scenario" and "2027-2029 Low Scenario". The y-axis is lifetime GHG savings in metric tons, from 0 to 1,800,000 in steps of 200,000. Stack segments (legend): Residential Programs (dark navy), C&I Programs (purple), DR Programs (blue), Non-EmPOWER Programs (green) and PIDD Programs (orange). Read visually (approximate; no data labels are printed): the 2025 bar totals about 1.7 million, with Residential about 0.68 million, C&I about 0.97 million and a thin green segment on top; the High Scenario bar totals about 1.69 million, with Residential about 0.9 million, C&I about 0.45 million and Non-EmPOWER (interconnected solar) about 0.34 million; the Low Scenario bar totals about 1.47 million, with Residential about 0.74 million, C&I about 0.44 million and Non-EmPOWER about 0.29 million. The C&I segment shrinks sharply from 2025 to the plan scenarios, which the text attributes to reduced claimable non-residential lighting savings.

[→ See original PDF page 32 for visual rendering]
The goal scenarios provided by Commission Order No. 92522 provided targets for
2027–2029 portfolio development. The reported historic 2025 energy savings
performance, translated to 2027–2029 emissions values, provided a base level of
performance to develop a 2027–2029 plan that met similar cost-effectiveness. BGE then
adjusted this starting base for portfolio performance up and down and to meet the

[PDF p. 33 | printed p. 23]

respective GHG goal scenarios. BGE further optimized the portfolio using information
from 2025 reporting to promote the most valuable measures in relation to dollars spent
per metric ton of GHG saved. This measure cost alignment heavily favored residential
space and water heating electrification projects, which are among the cheapest measures
available for GHG savings achievement. The increased emphasis on these measures,
particularly in the High Scenario, is further supported by growing participation trends seen
since the new offers were launched in 2025.
BGE is incorporating new measure offerings and program changes into the 2027–2029
program plan. These include consolidation of the Quick Home Energy Check-up (QHEC)
into the Home Performance Program, new electrification measure offerings in both the
residential and non-residential sectors, and updated incentive and rebate amounts. The
most notable change to the portfolio will be the capture of GHG emissions reductions
from interconnected solar installations that are connected to the distribution grid and
provide clean energy to BGE’s customers. Interconnected solar is projected to provide
20% of the portfolio’s GHG savings at no additional cost, which will deliver significant
affordability and environmental benefits to BGE’s customers.
BGE has also incorporated a residential new construction code compliance initiative into
the portfolio under the Residential New Construction Program. Code compliance offerings
in other state and utility jurisdictions have proven to provide significant and cost-effective
portfolio savings. These savings are typically captured three to five years from the
inception of the offer, depending on the jurisdictions’ code adoption cycle and timing of
program plan filings. We are including costs, but no savings, in this portfolio due to the
baseline code compliance studies and program implementation requirements. The latest
International Energy Conservation Code (IECC) is from 2024, and Maryland typically
adopts the latest IECC three years later. The first baseline year to examine the
compliance of new residential homes to IECC 2024 will be 2027. Implementation of
training programs to increase code compliance will take place in 2028 at the earliest and
the effect of those trainings will not be evident until the end of 2029 or later. This timeline
requires that budget be allocated for 2027–2029 and savings claimed in 2030–2033.
BGE also conducted significant research and analysis of existing energy efficiency and
electrification measures as well as previous program offerings to ensure a cost-efficient
(i.e., low cost per GHG reduced) portfolio design. We applied updated standards and
baselines to each measure offering as applicable and researched new measure offerings
to make sure that their energy savings potential was reasonable. BGE examined and
performed benchmarking of other jurisdictional offerings within and outside of the
Maryland utilities to provide new measure offerings. Some of these offerings include
additional electrification measures in the non-residential sector as well as updated

[PDF p. 34 | printed p. 24]

estimates for residential electrification offerings. Specific analysis of any new offerings
will be discussed in more detail in each offering’s respective program section.
The 2027–2029 program plan incorporates many of the potential new savings
methodologies from the upcoming version 12 of the TRM but maintaining alignment
between the portfolio designs and the numerous active drafts of TRM updates being
released during the planning period created additional complexity and challenges.
Updates to the draft TRM were released in March, April, May, and July with additional
future updates still pending as of the time of this filing. Each one of these updates changed
the results of our program modeling, requiring additional rebalancing of forecasts or
creating new evaluation risks. Since the latest version of the TRM has not yet been
published by the Independent Evaluator at the time of filing, it is not guaranteed that all
final TRM updates have been incorporated into the forecasts. These changes include
electrification energy estimates, updated efficiency baselines, and changes in heating and
cooling loads. Specifically, the calculation methodology for commercial electrification
projects remains undefined for a wide swath of applicable measures, especially larger
heat pump heating equipment systems. Discussions around these outstanding
calculations are ongoing. Our primary concern around this process is the lack of deadlines
for TRM updates, which creates a misalignment between what assumptions are used in
the modeling of the plans submitted and the numbers ultimately used by the Independent
Evaluator when the programs are evaluated later in the cycle.
Program evaluation results also influenced portfolio design and individual measure
planning. One particularly influential example of program evaluation effects is the results
from recent non-residential lighting program evaluations. These evaluations showed that,
due in part to successful implementation of past EmPOWER programs, the baseline of
non-residential lighting has increased, meaning that future implementation of C&I lighting
measures would provide diminished savings. Because of these diminished savings, the
contributions of non-residential lighting measures on the GHG emissions reductions
targets will be reduced by as much as 70% in Cycle 7 compared to Cycle 6. Much like the
reductions in residential lighting in past filings, non-residential lighting savings are a much
smaller proportion of the 2027–2029 portfolio plan.
While the two portfolios presented in this plan provide two cost-effective and optimized
scenarios, the development process was slowed as we awaited guidance and formal
goals from the Commission. Our initial portfolio development revolved around planning
equivalent performance to 2025 program actual results but scaling and improvements
could not be effectively performed during the initial months of development due to lack of
target metrics. The energy savings methodologies were also amorphous because the
latest version of the Maryland TRM is still in development at the time of this filing.

[PDF p. 35 | printed p. 25]

Significant changes to the TRM between versions 11 and 12 have influenced program
offerings, but the development timeline of TRM version 12 coincided with the portfolio
development, adding extra re-work and adjustment to some programs. Additionally, the
last-minute inclusion of two goal targets, which requires two portfolio scenarios, added
increased development time and resources.

#### 3.3.2 BGE Scenario Development

Commission Order No. 92522, published on July 10, 2026, requires utilities to submit two
portfolio plan scenarios reflecting two potential GHG emissions reduction goals. The High
Scenario Plan, which meets the goal resulting from the methodology proposed by
Commission Staff and MDE, was mainly developed in early 2026 by examining the 2024–
2026 EmPOWER filings and applying adjustments to program offerings and GHG
emissions. The Low Scenario Plan, which is based on a goal where the weighted average
EUL is seven, as was proposed by the Joint Exelon Utilities, was developed using historic
program performance and applying an average EUL based on the utilities’ historic mix of
measures implemented or installed by its customers.
The Low Scenario is met by removing some of the less effective program offerings across
the portfolio. Low Scenario budget savings are achieved through a combination of
program sunsetting, measure cuts within continuing programs, incentive and non-
incentive reductions, and reorganization of existing programs within the portfolio. It shifts
a significant portion of investment to electrification and home performance for residential
customers and allows non-residential programs to target the highest yield opportunities.
Moreover, this scenario removes measures that have seen stakeholder pushback due to
low cost-effectiveness or evaluation risk, as well as those that do not reflect current
market needs. The Low Scenario offers meaningful surcharge reduction without creating
significant gaps in BGE’s offerings—customers maintain access to efficiency incentives
across nearly all sectors, equipment types, and use cases. Overall, the Low Scenario
represents the streamlined demand-side management portfolio that BGE believes best
balances system benefits with customer cost savings and near-term surcharge relief.
Overall, the plan for the High Scenario largely continues BGE’s existing portfolio but with
the optimization described in the previous section. In the High Scenario, no programs are
entirely cut, except for Smart Energy Academy, with the intention of continuing to provide
as wide a range of efficiency options as possible for customers to participate in
EmPOWER which, in turn, gives BGE the greatest chance of meeting a higher goal.

[PDF p. 36 | printed p. 26]

#### 3.3.3 Innovative New Program Delivery Strategies

BGE is focused on improving the EmPOWER portfolio through innovative delivery
channels that enhance the customer experience and seek to find the projects that result
in the best savings. For the 2027–2029 cycle, BGE is proposing two key improvements:
increased coordination with DHCD on multifamily properties and customized, data-driven
marketing.

**Multifamily Customer Navigation Service**

BGE proposes a Multifamily Customer Navigation Service designed to improve customer
access to existing EmPOWER offerings through coordinated customer support, targeted
outreach, project coordination, and collaboration with DHCD. Rather than creating a new
multifamily program, dedicated incentives, or a separate funding pool, this proposal
establishes a coordinated customer pathway that helps multifamily property owners and
managers identify and access the existing programs best suited to their property’s needs.
The proposal addresses a long-standing challenge within the multifamily market. Unlike
traditional residential or commercial customers, multifamily property owners and
managers often must navigate multiple participation pathways before determining which
programs apply to their property. Eligibility may depend on several factors, including:

- Customer eligibility for DHCD-administered multifamily offerings
- Building configuration and metering arrangements
- Whether common areas and individual dwelling units qualify for different
  programs

- Residential versus commercial utility program requirements
- Split incentives between property owners and residents
- Opportunities that span multiple existing programs and service providers
  The Multifamily Customer Navigation Service simplifies this experience by providing a
  dedicated multifamily resource that serves as a single point of contact for property owners
  and managers. Rather than requiring customers to independently navigate the complex
  landscape of DHCD and utility-administered programs, eligibility requirements,
  participation pathways, and service providers, the dedicated resource will coordinate
  these activities on the customer's behalf and connect customers with the opportunities
  best suited to their property's needs.

[PDF p. 37 | printed p. 27]

As Maryland’s lead agency for affordable housing, DHCD will continue to determine
eligibility and administer programs serving qualifying multifamily properties. BGE
recognizes and supports DHCD’s role in serving this market.
Under the proposed framework:

- A dedicated multifamily resource will conduct targeted outreach to multifamily
  property owners and managers.

- The dedicated multifamily resource will provide education, project coordination,
  and navigation support to help customers understand available opportunities.

- BGE will coordinate with DHCD to determine eligibility for DHCD-administered
  multifamily offerings.

- Properties that qualify for DHCD-administered offerings will be referred to DHCD
  for program participation.

- Properties that do not qualify will be connected to the appropriate BGE-
  administered EmPOWER programs.

- Where opportunities exist across multiple programs, the dedicated multifamily
  resource will help customers navigate those pathways to maximize participation
  while avoiding duplication of effort.
  This coordinated approach creates a seamless customer experience, reduces confusion
  regarding program eligibility, prevents customers from being turned away, strengthens
  collaboration between BGE and DHCD, and helps ensure multifamily customers are
  connected to the existing resources best suited to their property's needs.
  It also provides an opportunity to introduce multifamily customers to additional utility-
  administered offerings, including multi-unit dwelling EV programs, future DRIVE
  initiatives, and other programs that support electrification, building modernization,
  affordability, and customer choice.
  Importantly, this proposal does not:

- Create a standalone multifamily program.
- Establish dedicated multifamily incentives.
- Create a separate multifamily funding pool.
- Duplicate, replace, or interfere with DHCD-administered multifamily offerings.
- Change DHCD's role as the lead agency serving eligible affordable multifamily
  properties.

- Create a competing pathway for properties that qualify for DHCD-administered
  programs.

[PDF p. 38 | printed p. 28]

By providing a dedicated multifamily resource as a single point of contact, this proposal
simplifies an otherwise complex customer experience. Rather than requiring multifamily
property owners and managers to independently navigate program eligibility
requirements, participation pathways, and multiple service providers, the service helps
connect customers with the appropriate opportunities while preserving DHCD's role as
Maryland's lead agency for affordable multifamily housing.

**Data-Driven Customer Engagement to Maximize Portfolio Benefits**

With affordability top of mind during the planning process for the upcoming cycle, BGE is
building on its existing marketing approach by applying an additional analysis to help
maximize impact with available resources.
In the 2027–2029 cycle, BGE proposes using this lens to further inform how marketing
and incentive dollars are prioritized, with a focus on projects that deliver strong benefits
for customers, EmPOWER’s GHG goals, and the grid. Factors that will be examined
include:

- Cost-effectiveness, such as dollars spent per GHG reduction achieved
- Customer benefits, such as estimated bill savings per participating customer
- Grid benefits, such as peak demand reduction or avoided system costs
  This initiative will begin with data analysis that complements existing program and
  marketing planning. The BGE team will review high-benefit projects within each program
  to determine whether common customer or project characteristics are associated with
  particularly strong outcomes. This approach is intended to be customer-first rather than
  program-specific; while the analysis may begin at the program level, the resulting insights
  would be used to identify customer needs and connect customers to the program,
  measure, or solution that best fits their circumstances.
  Once meaningful characteristics are identified, BGE will use those insights to refine
  targeted marketing strategies that connect customers with the most relevant program
  options based on their needs and characteristics. Strategies will include leveraging
  customer segments, applying propensity models, analyzing media consumption habits,
  aligning with paid media strategy, using data collected in virtual audits, and supporting
  contractors or other trusted messengers who influence customer decision-making.
  Tactics would be considered through the customer journey to build awareness, educate
  customers, and drive participation.

[PDF p. 39 | printed p. 29]

For non-residential customer characteristics identified through data analysis, the outreach
team will layer direct engagement onto broader marketing efforts, such as phone calls or
site visits, to explain relevant program benefits and solutions. For neighborhoods or
geographic areas that share certain characteristics, BGE will provide participating service
providers with information and marketing collateral to support customer education and
sales conversations.
One example of a similar strategy utilized in 2026 is BGE’s identification of residential
customers who heat their homes with propane or fuel oil and may see significant energy
savings from electrification projects. This insight helped inform targeted email and direct
mail encouraging these customers to explore electrification benefits available through
several residential energy efficiency programs.
Ultimately, BGE plans to further leverage data analysis and targeted marketing as an
added strategy to maximize EmPOWER Program benefits and help connect customers
with the solutions that can provide the greatest value. This initiative is not intended to
create new offerings, provide additional incentives, or replace existing marketing
practices; rather, it is intended to enhance and inform existing efforts.

### 3.4 Alignment With Other Regulatory Dockets

BGE’s EmPOWER implementation team sits within the larger Regulatory and Strategy
division, allowing for close collaboration between regulatory dockets and coordination
between regulatorily required programs.

#### 3.4.1 Drive Act

The DRIVE Act Pilot and EmPOWER load management programs are increasingly
interconnected as distributed energy resources, customer-owned technologies, and
flexible load solutions continue to evolve. Although the programs are governed by
separate statutory frameworks, many of the same customer technologies have the
potential to support the objectives of both initiatives when implemented within a
supportive regulatory structure.
In Case No. 9761, stakeholders have already discussed the potential benefits of
transitioning EmPOWER load management programs to the DRIVE Act[^p39n11] framework,
including opportunities for improved customer experience, operational efficiencies, and
program coordination. The Commission has also indicated that such a transition could be

[^p39n11]: See ML326404, section 2.4.1 EmPOWER Maryland and ML328165 Case 9761 Report Section 1: Proposal to Move EmPOWER to DRIVE Act

[PDF p. 40 | printed p. 30]

considered as part of a future filing to move a pilot to a permanent program at the
conclusion of the DRIVE Act pilot period. Rather than revisiting those broader policy
discussions here, BGE's focus is on the practical customer and operational
considerations that arise from the interaction between the EmPOWER and DRIVE Act
programs.
As opportunities for customers to engage with DER programs expands, BGE anticipates
an increase in customers who are likely to participate with multiple DER types, potentially
requiring customers to be involved in multiple programs spread across various regulatory
proceedings for load management including EmPOWER Maryland demand response
programs, DRIVE Act pilots, and Smart Charge Management. Without coordination, these
resources may be independently optimized for different, specific objectives despite the
intention of serving the same distribution system.
This high likelihood of a single customer participating in a variety of programs creates
both opportunities and challenges. On one hand, the combination of thermostats, direct
load control devices, batteries, electric vehicles, and other flexible resources creates a
more capable virtual power plant (VPP) that can improve trust in customer-sited
resources by providing durable support during system events. Longer, more durable
support means increased confidence in the VPP’s ability to deliver time after time. On the
other hand, maintaining separate administrative structures, reporting requirements,
dispatch mechanisms, customer incentives, and technology platforms may create
unnecessary complexity for customers and limit the ability to optimize resources for
maximum system value.
From an operational perspective, a unified orchestration framework provides significant
advantages. Distribution operators must increasingly understand available load flexibility
across the grid and dispatch participating resources in a coordinated manner. Managing
flexible loads through multiple program structures can limit visibility into available
resources and reduce opportunities for optimized dispatch strategies. Put simply, the
programs serve multiple outcomes instead of working toward the same targets.
Consolidated management of controllable resources would support more efficient event
execution, improve situational awareness, and enable BGE to maximize realized peak
reductions while preserving customer experience.
The interaction between the programs is also important from a customer perspective.
Customers generally do not distinguish between EmPOWER, DRIVE, PJM market
participation, or distribution planning objectives. Rather, customers expect a simple and
consistent participation experience that provides clear value and avoids disparate
enrollment pathways. As additional DER technologies become available, preserving a

[PDF p. 41 | printed p. 31]

cohesive customer experience will become increasingly important to maintaining
participation and maximizing adoption across all customer classes. Without sustained
participation, customer-owned and participating DER technologies will not be able to
provide system support in a way that would result in achieving Commission-stated goals
for the distribution system.
BGE acknowledges and has already started functioning under the understanding that
close coordination between the DRIVE Act and EmPOWER proceedings will be
necessary to avoid duplication, improve administrative efficiency, and ensure programs
remain aligned with their intended policy objectives. Such coordination will become
increasingly critical as Maryland's demand response, energy storage, electrification, and
VPP initiatives continue to mature. BGE continues to seek operational efficiencies to
provide a single point of orchestration for DER and customer-owned load flexibility
resources.

#### 3.4.2 UBCA Working Group

The Unified Benefit Cost Analysis (UBCA) Working Group is tasked with creating a unified
methodology that can be utilized to determine the costs and benefits of DERs in Maryland,
including those that participate in or are impacted by EmPOWER programs. Because of
this, the same inputs utilized in the UBCA will be used in the EmPOWER Benefit Cost
Analysis (BCA) and cost effectiveness analysis. The UBCA Working Group met regularly
during the same time period as a subgroup of the EmPOWER Evaluation Advisory Group
(EAG) was developing the avoided cost analysis for the 2027–2029 cycle. As changes
were made in the UBCA, the EAG subgroup sought to align the EmPOWER
methodologies. For each of the impacts, the UBCA consultant team provided a timeline
for implementation that included either immediate or interim methods to be used. As we
move into the 2027–2029 cycle, changes have been made to the following avoided costs
analysis inputs:

- Avoided Energy – The approach to this input in EmPOWER, while following
  some of the UBCA’s recommendations, will differ primarily due to the MDE
  emissions assumptions being incorporated into the value stream instead of
  purely using the NLR Cambium values as the UBCA team recommended.

- Avoided Electric Generation Capacity – The estimated timeline to establish the
  final modeling of this input is over two years so, in the interim, the EmPOWER
  analysis will use previous EmPOWER assumptions or NLR Cambium values.

- Avoided Transmission & Distribution (T&D) – The UBCA recommended utilizing
  a cost to load growth method to estimate system-wide impacts, which is
  projected to take six to twelve months to implement. The interim method is to use
  current EmPOWER assumptions, resulting in T&D values simply increasing with
  inflation.

[PDF p. 42 | printed p. 32]

- Energy Demand Response Induced Price Effects (DRIPE) – The UBCA team
  recommended a methodology which analyzes the price to demand relationship
  using market price data. Accordingly, EmPOWER analysis will use this market
  price data as part of the avoided energy cost development and increase the
  impact from four to ten years.

- Gas Energy – The UBCA recommendation for gas energy impacts differs
  substantially from the previous EmPOWER cycle, utilizing a Henry Hub plus
  basis adder instead of the previously used NYMEX forecast.

- Gas Capacity – The UBCA recommends calculating a cost to load growth
  method that would take between six and twelve months to complete. Therefore,
  EmPOWER will utilize an interim method of taking zero impact.

- Gas Market Price Effects – The UBCA team believed that gas DRIPE impacts
  are not material and should not be used. Gas DRIPE was a small impact on the
  previous cycle and will not be used at all for the 2027–2029 cycle.

### 3.5 Implementation Strategy

#### 3.5.1 Minimizing Administrative Costs

BGE’s focus on customer affordability applies to all aspects of program design and
implementation, including both incentive and non-incentive costs. In Order No. 92522, the
Commission encouraged parties to work together to improve the utilities’ incentive to
administrative cost ratio where possible in the 2027–2029 program plan filings. Both
scenarios presented in this filing achieve an incentive to non-incentive budget ratio of
approximately 70%, inclusive of all programs, even those that do not provide incentives.
Methods of appropriately including or excluding costs for these purposes are being
developed in the EAG for uniform application across all utilities.
BGE scaled budgets between scenarios based on the underlying program and portfolio
assumptions instead of using a uniform adjustment factor. Programs that did not
materially change between scenarios kept budgets the same, while administrative and
implementation costs were adjusted to reflect scenario-specific program activity and
program size. In some cases, we allocated program non-incentive budgets using
percentages of total portfolio spending to maintain consistency with overall planning
assumptions. The planning team also reviewed utility administration and incentive costs
separately to ensure that expenditure remained aligned with forecasted program delivery
and participation levels. EM&V costs were generally scaled based on a percentage of
overall program spend.
BGE carefully reviewed administrative costs throughout the planning process to ensure
that portfolio spending remained focused on customer-facing program delivery and

[PDF p. 43 | printed p. 33]

energy savings. Utility administration budgets were generally held constant between
scenarios for programs that did not have significant design or participant forecast
changes. Proposed adjustments to administrative costs were evaluated to ensure they
were directly tied to program implementation needs. The planning team also reviewed
administrative cost ratios across scenarios to maintain consistency and cost-
effectiveness. This approach helped minimize administrative spending while ensuring
that the budgets would be sufficient to deliver portfolio objectives and achieve program
goals.

#### 3.5.2 EmPOWER Utility Coordination

BGE has maintained a strong commitment to cross-utility collaboration throughout the
EmPOWER Maryland planning process. We have actively hosted and moderated monthly
cross-utility coordination calls, as well as ad hoc conversations as topics arise, to align
strategic priorities, share best practices, and identify opportunities for collaboration and
alignment. Through these ongoing engagements, BGE has helped foster a coordinated
approach across Maryland utilities, ensuring that key stakeholders remain informed,
connected, and positioned to address common challenges and opportunities as we
prepare for the 2027–2029 EmPOWER cycle filing.

#### 3.5.3 Adaptation and Flexibility

Flexibility has been a core aspect of planning for the next EmPOWER cycle. During the
development of the Cycle 7 goals, the GHG emissions calculations, emissions factors,
and goal targets were subject to several different iterations. During this time, BGE
examined its existing programs and continued to develop program plans even while these
metrics were changing. Program evaluations and TRM changes also were happening
during the planning period and BGE made swift updates to the program planning process
under tight timelines to reflect those updates as they occurred. Finally, late into the plan
development process, BGE was required to develop two scenarios for filing, which
required tracking differences in not only program metrics but also the effects to customer
bills through the EmPOWER surcharge.
Similarly, flexibility will be a critical resource to facilitate implementation moving forward.
EmPOWER Cycle 7 represents the first full cycle with an established lifecycle GHG goal.
As a result, BGE’s EmPOWER programs are still adapting to meet this new paradigm.
This has resulted in both proposed portfolios expanding electrification across all sectors
and otherwise emphasizing long-life measures to the greatest extent possible. The
significant changes expected across a number of programs create substantial risk that
there will be unpredicted barriers and setbacks. Regardless of selected scenario,
implementation may need to adapt by updating program delivery strategies or budgets

[PDF p. 44 | printed p. 34]

mid-cycle, as approved by the Commission. This flexibility is critical to allowing BGE to
fulfill its EmPOWER commitments and create a smooth customer experience wherever
possible.
Throughout the fluctuations in planning goal development, program evaluations, and TRM
development, BGE has been able to improvise, adapt, and develop a set of program
plans. Each of these plans will meet the regulatory goals of GHG reduction, promote
energy efficiency, maintain reliability, and improve customer affordability.

#### 3.5.4 Data Analytics and Internal Evaluation

During implementation of this portfolio, BGE and its contracted vendors are committed to
collecting as much data as is reasonable and constantly using it to ensure cost-effective
and impactful delivery of programs and measures. This includes internal savings reviews,
ongoing reviews of measure level cost-effectiveness and subsequent tuning of incentive
levels, ongoing coordination with EM&V vendors, and any other analytics necessary to
BGE maintaining an effective portfolio.

## 4. Residential Portfolio

### 4.1 Portfolio Overview

Noting the transition to GHG goals during Cycle 6 (2024–2026 cycle), the proposed
residential portfolio seeks to build on the adjustments that were made to optimize GHG
savings as well as BGE’s early success in electrification offerings. The Residential
Portfolio seeks to add window heat pumps as a new measure as well as to incorporate
codes and standards into the New Construction Program. BGE continues to recognize
that the EmPOWER environment continues to evolve with changing standards and new
technologies. Feedback from continued engagement with Maryland stakeholders, utility
peers across the U.S. and Canada, innovators, and industry group best practices were
used in the development of the Residential Portfolio.
BGE is proposing to offer the following programs during Cycle 7 (2027–2029 cycle):

- Efficient Products
  o Appliance Recycling
  o Appliance Rebates

- Home Optimization and Retrofit
  o Residential HVAC

[PDF p. 45 | printed p. 35]

o Home Upgrade

- Residential New Construction
- Smart Energy Manager
  While many of the program names are the same, these programs are constantly evolving
  to meet the needs of our customers and the environment we operate in. BGE continues
  to implement successful data analytics and delivery tools to better inform decisions for
  program modifications, drive the productivity of trade allies, encourage greater
  participation, and support evaluators in validating claimed energy savings. In the
  upcoming cycle BGE looks to further enhance pathways for customer participation,
  helping them to make informed choices along their “EmPOWERed” journey.
  The BGE Smart Energy Academy Program will be sunset at the end of Cycle 6. The
  program savings diminished as EmPOWER shifted from annual MWh savings to lifecycle
  GHG savings. BGE explored alternative programs and measures to increase savings but
  decided to focus on other programs in the portfolio that deliver more lifecycle GHG
  savings.
  In the following sections, we describe our approach to evolving our successful portfolio of
  programs, leveraging lessons learned and successful strategies and best practices from
  similar programs across the U.S. and Canada, and continuing the refinement of programs
  through stakeholder input, applied research, and evaluation feedback. We also note
  differences in each program between the two scenarios.
  BGE’s Residential Portfolio has been an industry leader in the shift to a GHG-focused
  portfolio as well as in our electrification offerings, and we look to build on this success in
  Cycle 7.

#### 4.1.1 Residential Portfolio Marketing

Residential energy efficiency marketing embraces a holistic strategy designed to support
GHG emissions reduction goals, including electrification. The marketing leverages
multichannel campaigns to boost customer awareness and program participation,
educate customers about energy efficiency and available programs, engage key
segments, reach target audiences, and drive recognition through attention-grabbing
campaign creative and messaging.
For the 2027–2029 program cycle, several strategic residential themes may shape
individual program marketing and outreach plans while supporting broader residential
energy efficiency marketing objectives.

[PDF p. 46 | printed p. 36]

First, a creative concept that leverages customer research and territory-specific insights
may be woven throughout residential marketing creative. These campaigns will target
residential customers as well as additional key audience segments, including contractors,
distributors, homebuilders, and others who influence energy efficiency decisions.
Next, leading up to extreme temperature seasons when energy use typically spikes,
marketing may include energy efficiency tips and education with a focus on easy home
adjustments that save energy and on learning more about high-efficiency equipment.
Specific residential program solutions may be woven throughout.
Additionally, this program cycle may include an emphasis on featuring real, relatable
customer, contractor, and partner stories. Sharing authentic experiences builds trust in
program offerings and shows local people in recognizable places benefiting from energy
efficiency solutions. We may share these stories through articles, videos, interviews,
social media, BGE’s website, and utility-owned channels. The stories may be
complemented and amplified by coordination with BGE Communications, offering
opportunities to reach other channels such as news outlets.
Marketing efforts may also use residential customer insights, past participation data, and
technology solutions to deliver personalized energy efficiency recommendations that fit
each customer’s home and lifestyle. Examples may include digital quiz tools that guide
customers to eligible programs, personalized emails based on indicated interests, and
follow-up direct marketing that offers recent participants the best next step in their energy
efficiency journey. BGE’s website will also be kept current and optimized with features
such as chat and enhanced search, helping customers quickly find relevant solutions.
We may weave cross-promotions throughout residential program marketing. Participation
data and customer behavior can be used to identify relevant program pathways, including
opportunities for customers to take a next step, participate again if eligible, or re-engage
after previous participation.
Finally, for the contractors, homebuilders, distributors, outreach professionals, and other
stakeholders who play critical roles in identifying energy efficiency opportunities,
marketing may continue to provide program resources and collateral to support partner
engagement and facilitate customer participation.

[PDF p. 47 | printed p. 37]

### 4.2 Efficient Products (Appliance Rebates, Appliance Recycling)

#### 4.2.1 Program Overview

The BGE Efficient Products Program is comprised of two components: the
BGE Residential Appliance Rebate offering and the BGE Residential Appliance
Recycling offering.
The BGE Residential Appliance Rebate component encourages consumers to
purchase higher efficiency appliances by offering incentives for select ENERGY STAR
certified products through multiple sales channels. The offering first launched in 2008 and
offered rebate incentives for ENERGY STAR product measures including refrigerators,
clothes washers, and room air conditioners. During the 2024–2026 cycle, the Appliance
Rebate Program provided incentives through a multitude of delivery channels including
the ENERGY STAR Retail Products Platform (ESRPP); verified instant, post-purchase,
direct-to-consumer kits distributed via non-profit organizations; instant markdowns; and
midstream via distributors. ESRPP incentives are not paid to customers; they are an
upstream incentive paid to retailers to encourage and support energy-efficient
manufacture and retailer stocking habits. BGE requests flexibility to augment delivery
channels and measures (along with their respective incentive caps) offered within the
program during Cycle 7 to react to market adoption and to best serve customers.
Because the incentives are more reflective of the purchasing trends of each measure,
this flexibility will allow BGE to more efficiently manage spending and deliver greater
benefits to our customers.
The BGE Residential Appliance Recycling component offers residential customers
the opportunity to recycle their inefficient operational appliances. BGE was an
EPA Responsible Appliance Disposal (RAD) Partner until EPA sunset the program in
2025. The BGE Appliance Recycling Program ensures that all appliances are recycled
in an environmentally responsible way that continues to meet the former EPA RAD
standards. BGE residential customers can schedule an appliance pickup appointment to
turn in large appliances (refrigerators or freezers) as well as up to two room air
conditioners, compact refrigerators, or dehumidifiers at the same time. BGE maximizes
program accessibility by ensuring sufficient appointment availability and hosting
additional recycling turn-in events throughout the year. Turn-in events allow customers to
recycle a small unit without a large unit pickup appointment. Participating customers then
receive an incentive for each recycled appliance removed from the grid.
The BGE Residential Appliance Recycling Program leverages the retail partnerships
developed by the Appliance Rebate Program. Point of purchase (POP) materials help
advertise the Appliance Recycling offering in participating retail locations. The Appliance

[PDF p. 48 | printed p. 38]

Recycling offering also partners with 26 Home Depot stores in the BGE service territory
to provide the opportunity to schedule recycling pickup appointments at the time of
purchase. This scheduling option offers pickup of the old appliance when the new
appliance is delivered.
During the 2027–2029 program cycle, the BGE Residential Appliance Recycling
offering will continue to maintain the same structure, including small commercial
refrigerator and freezer pickups and a midstream recycling channel through second-hand
appliance retailers. The midstream channel officially launched in 2026 with the goal to
remove inefficient secondhand appliances from these retailers so they do not get resold
and reintroduced to the electric grid.

#### 4.2.2 Goal Scenario Modifications

In the Low Scenario, the Appliance Recycling offer will be sunset for the 2027–2029
program cycle due to its relatively small contribution to overall lifecycle GHG savings as
well as the labor-intensive administration of the service.

#### 4.2.3 Target Market

The BGE Residential Appliance Rebate offering is available to all residential BGE account
holders in the market for new appliances. The BGE Residential Appliance Recycling
offering targets BGE residential customers and small commercial customers with old and
inefficient working appliances. In the upcoming cycle, the Appliance Recycling Program
will continue to focus on both single-family and multifamily households, small businesses,
and commercial offices in addition to customers purchasing or replacing appliances at
retail.

#### 4.2.4 Market Analysis and Trends

Appliance measures are playing a more significant role in residential programs due to the
removal of most lighting products as viable energy efficiency measures. To achieve the
necessary savings from appliance measures, it is important to offer incentives through a
number of delivery channels.

#### 4.2.5 Eligible Measures/Services

The BGE Residential Appliance Rebate Program will offer incentives through several
channels on a number of residential products to potentially include refrigerators, freezers,
clothes washers, clothes dryers, room air conditioners, room air purifiers, dehumidifiers,
heat pump water heaters (HPWHs), smart thermostats, advanced power strips, induction
cooktops, select lighting products, and ceiling fans. BGE may explore other energy-
efficient products during the program cycle. We will research additional possible
measures for viability and incentivize them with an amount informed by market research.
Where possible, smart thermostats will be enabled for pre-enrollment in Connected
Rewards.
To increase the electrification of appliances, the Appliance Rebate Program is focusing
on increasing the adoption of HPWHs. A key element for HPWH market growth and
adaptation across all residential programs is the planning and implementation of an
education and outreach campaign to support sales growth across all sales channels,
overcome market barriers, and increase customer, contractor, distributor, and builder
awareness. BGE launched this effort in 2026.
The BGE Residential Appliance Recycling Program is designed to provide customer
convenience and allow customers to easily participate without having to leave home. The
program accepts the following working appliances for recycling:

- Refrigerators
- Freezers

[PDF p. 50 | printed p. 40]

- Room Air Conditioners
- Dehumidifiers
- Compact Refrigerators

#### 4.2.7 Marketing

**Appliance Rebates Program**

The BGE Appliance Rebates Program marketing strategy for 2027–2029 will continue to
emphasize the appliances that present the greatest opportunity for GHG reductions, while
still supporting marketing for other higher efficiency appliances. Marketing will highlight
available rebates and long-term energy savings. Timing will focus on advertising during
key seasonality periods, as needed. For example, air purifiers may be more relevant in
the spring while dehumidifiers may receive more marketing emphasis during hot and
humid summer months. HPWHs, due to continuous water heater replacement needs and
strong impact on program goals, could be promoted year-round.
In addition to marketing during key seasons, program marketing strategies may also
include holiday promotions aligned in timing with retail partners, thus layering together
the effects of rebates and retail sales to maximize customer savings. Lastly, BGE may
use limited-time offers to drive additional participation, as needed.
The BGE Residential Appliance Recycling Program relies heavily on marketing for
continued program success. The marketing strategy includes sustained year-round
advertising coupled with seasonal marketing pushes during three key periods:

- Spring: Customers are engaged in spring cleaning and home improvements.
  They are motivated to declutter.

- Early Summer: This is another typically high-volume season when customers
  may be noticing room air conditioners, dehumidifiers, or other appliances that are
  in need of replacement ahead of summer heat and humidity.

- Late Summer: Participation spikes again in September, with back-to-school
  organization and preparation.
  Apart from seasonal marketing pushes, consistent presence at retail locations with
  in-store POP signage and brochures also drives program participation, either through

[PDF p. 51 | printed p. 41]

customers browsing in the store and picking up brochures or buying a new refrigerator or
freezer and being met with advertising to recycle their old one.
Limited-time offer promotions have also proven to be an effective strategy for increasing
recycling pickup volume. By creating a sense of urgency and encouraging customers to
recycle aging appliances sooner, these promotions have consistently generated
customer engagement.
Recycling drop-off event promotions provide another avenue for program participation
and community engagement. Community partners often allow for the opportunity to cross-
promote and advertise events together, thus amplifying the collective turnout and impact
on number of units recycled.
Finally, in addition to residential customer marketing, BGE will provide information and
resources to appliance retailers to encourage recycling of old, working appliances through
the midstream channel.
BGE will deploy a variety of marketing tactics for Appliance Recycling, including
broadcast radio, streaming audio and video, digital marketing, social media, content
marketing, direct mail, email, retail rack cards and other POP, strategic partnerships, and
utility-owned channels.

### 4.3 Residential HVAC

#### 4.3.1 Program Overview

The BGE Residential HVAC Program provides incentives for the purchase of
high-efficiency space and water heating equipment. The program is intended to drive
savings for customers and promote heat pump adoption in the BGE service territory. In
the last program cycle, BGE launched new incentives for electrification projects in the
program. These offers contributed to BGE’s year-over-year growth in air source heat
pump adoption, creating a strong foundation for Maryland’s long-term market
transformation ambitions. As a result, the HVAC Program has become the portfolio’s
leading contributor to GHG savings. This 2027–2029 program cycle will endeavor to
continue to evolve the Residential HVAC Program.

[PDF p. 52 | printed p. 42]

The program is designed to operate via a midstream model whereby incentives are paid
to the distributor, who offers discounts on the qualifying equipment to encourage
contractors to purchase. The program will engage distributors, contractors, local market
trade groups, industry associations, manufacturers, and other market actors through
direct program outreach, marketing advertisements, and marketing outreach campaigns.
This will continue to drive program participation and will further transform the market as
distributors, contractors, and end customers promote and purchase high-efficiency HVAC
equipment.
The success of this program relies on an established streamlined experience for all
parties involved, from education through execution. BGE will continue to work with
stakeholders and explore potential new mechanisms to facilitate contractor and customer
participation in BGE’s successful Residential HVAC Program.

#### 4.3.2 Goal Scenario Modifications

In the Low Scenario, the Residential HVAC Program budget and forecast follows the
trajectory of 2026 production trends providing proven, high value (dollar per GHG)
savings. This moderate growth trajectory supports overall reductions to the residential
surcharge by providing more savings for less program cost and reduces EM&V risk
related to any future changes to these high-impact measures. In the High Scenario, the
budget and forecast includes significant electrification growth. This growth provides the
opportunity to achieve the increased GHG emissions reduction goal of the High Scenario
at a significantly lower cost compared to other measures across the portfolio but also
introduces risk with a very high proportion of portfolio savings coming from this single
program.

| --- | --- | --- |

| Low Scenario | $86,682,592 | 830,274 |
| Difference | $54,102,080 | 278,567 |

#### 4.3.3 Target Market

The target market includes BGE service area homeowners with an existing operating
HVAC system (particularly older systems) or those that need an immediate system
replacement.

[PDF p. 53 | printed p. 43]

#### 4.3.4 Market Analysis and Trends

The BGE Residential HVAC Program began transitioning to the midstream model in Q4
2018 and moved all unitary measures to midstream by January 1, 2020, incentivizing
manufacturers and distributors to discount the sale price of energy-efficient models when
sold to contractors. To leverage the success of the midstream program, BGE will add
HPWH measures to the HVAC measure list in the 2027–2029 program cycle. Air source
heat pumps, with hybrid, ductless, or ducted models, are leading the decarbonization
transition by putting customer choice first. Cold climate heat pumps operate effectively in
colder temperatures, reducing the need for backup fossil fuel or electric resistance heat.
Air source heat pump technology gives customers ultimate control over how they heat
and cool their homes.

#### 4.3.5 Eligible Measures/Services

The measures in this program include air source heat pumps, cold climate heat pumps,
ductless (single and multi-zone mini split) heat pumps, ground source heat pumps,
HPWHs, and smart thermostats. All systems will meet minimum measure eligibility
requirements as listed in the AHRI database. Under the midstream program structure,
distributors are provided with the BGE HVAC equipment incentive structure as a baseline
to develop their own internal program designs, which encourage the delivery of high-
efficiency heating and cooling equipment to BGE customers. Direct payment to
participating distributors will still be set by program management per measure.

#### 4.3.6 Customer Incentives

See Attachment 2 measures and incentives.
Proper incentive levels are designed to bridge the cost gap between efficient and
standard products. BGE determines incentives through ongoing market analysis and
consultation with local distributors. We will monitor and adjust incentive levels throughout
the program cycle to protect against over-incentivizing or under-incentivizing measures.

#### 4.3.7 Marketing

The marketing for HVAC will continue to promote the benefits of energy-efficient heating
and cooling equipment, as well as additional incentives available when switching from
natural gas, oil, or propane to electric systems. HVAC marketing will target both contractor
and customer audience segments. Seasonality will influence marketing, with more
education deployed during peak summer cooling months and peak winter heating
months.

[PDF p. 54 | printed p. 44]

In addition to aligning with seasonality, BGE will activate a variety of marketing strategies,
including education on priority equipment and electrification, augmenting customer and
contractor online research, and supporting distributors with education, training materials,
collateral, and POP signage.
Marketing tactics will include streaming video, digital marketing, social media, content
marketing, direct mail, email, and utility-owned channels.
