### 4.4 Home Upgrade (Legacy Home Performance & Quick Home Energy Checkup)

For the 2027–2029 cycle, BGE will introduce a comprehensively redesigned Home
Upgrade Program that consolidates multiple residential programs and offerings within a
single cohesive program structure. This unified program framework will provide
customers with flexible pathways to pursue home energy efficiency and electrification
upgrades, backed by a network of experienced home improvement contractors and
licensed professionals. By consolidating the prior structure of standalone offerings, the
Home Upgrade Program will reduce program complexity to better support customers
throughout their retrofit journey from initial education and light-touch services to
comprehensive, whole-home efficiency and electrification upgrades.
The redesign reflects both changing customer expectations and evolving market
conditions. Customers increasingly seek simplified program experiences, comprehensive
home solutions, and flexible pathways that accommodate projects completed over
multiple years. At the same time, traditional direct-install offerings have produced
diminishing savings as efficient lighting and other low-cost energy-saving opportunities
have become increasingly commonplace. The redesigned program positions BGE to
deliver deeper energy savings, greater GHG reductions, and a more streamlined
customer experience while reducing administrative complexity.

**Moderate Income Expansion:** BGE recognizes that many customers experience
affordability challenges and face barriers to participating in comprehensive energy
efficiency and electrification projects despite not qualifying for traditional income-qualified
programs. To help address this gap, BGE proposes enhanced support for moderate-
income customers through enhanced incentive offerings and prescriptive-style incentive
packages designed to reduce first-cost barriers for high-impact measures. These

[PDF p. 55 | printed p. 45]

offerings are intended to expand access to deeper home energy improvements that
provide meaningful bill savings, comfort benefits, and GHG reductions.
Regarding the moderate-income offer, the Commission Order No. 92522 asks “whether
or not a broader income threshold as recommended by OPC is appropriate." BGE
remains open to continued stakeholder discussions regarding the appropriate income
eligibility thresholds for these offerings and will evaluate opportunities to expand
participation to additional customers if doing so can be achieved in a cost-effective
manner and complement existing DHCD and MEA programs while minimizing any
potential confusion for these customers.

#### 4.4.1 Program Overview

BGE’s Home Upgrade Program will transition to a fully integrated, end-to-end residential
offering designed to deliver a continuous pathway from initial customer engagement
through comprehensive whole-home energy upgrades. This program will integrate and
modernize elements of BGE’s legacy Quick Home Energy Check-up (QHEC), Home
Performance with ENERGY STAR (HPwES), Programs and HVAC Tune-up offer into a
unified set of customer-facing offerings that is easier to navigate and better supports
customers throughout their decision-making process. The HVAC Tune-up offer
currently resides in the HVAC Residential Program.
Under this design, BGE will sunset the standalone QHEC offering and incorporate its
educational and direct-install components into a broader home retrofit framework.
Customers will be able to access a continuum of engagement options ranging from light-
touch educational in-home visits and virtual assessments to comprehensive contractor-
delivered retrofit projects. This approach allows customers to enter the program at
multiple points depending on their needs while ensuring a clear pathway toward deeper
savings opportunities over time.
This coordinated model reduces friction throughout the customer journey and improves
overall program accessibility while maintaining a clear and consistent pathway to
comprehensive energy upgrades. Administrative resources previously required to
operate separate program tracks will be restructured to support a unified program.
The Home Upgrade Program emphasizes a whole-home, building systems approach.
The program prioritizes improvements to the building envelope, including air sealing and
insulation, as the foundation for achieving sustained energy savings, GHG reductions,
and comfort benefits. Building on this foundation, the program will continue to support
replacement of existing heating and cooling systems with high-efficiency heat pump

[PDF p. 56 | printed p. 46]

equipment. Importantly, this includes replacing existing fossil fuel heating systems with
high-efficiency electric heat pumps—which are particularly effective when paired
with building envelope and weatherization upgrades.
Program delivery will be contractor-led and customer-centric, supported by integrated
tools, streamlined workflows, and flexible incentives that encourage deeper savings and
high-quality installations. Customers will receive tailored recommendations informed by
diagnostic testing, assessment results, and contractor expertise, with clear information
regarding project costs, expected savings, and available incentives.
Consistent with prior program design, incentives will continue to reward verified energy
savings outcomes and comprehensive project scopes. BGE will maintain flexibility to
evolve program offerings in response to market conditions, stakeholder feedback,
technology improvements, and potential partnership opportunities with community-based
organizations and external funding sources.

**Key Changes:**

**Transition to a Unified Program Structure:** BGE will combine multiple legacy offerings,
including QHEC, HPwES, and HVAC Tune-up, into a coordinated Home Upgrade
Program. This integrated framework improves coordination across program pathways
and enables a more seamless customer experience.

**Evolution of the Traditional QHEC Model:** The standalone QHEC offering will be
incorporated into the broader Home Upgrade Program structure. While QHEC has
delivered nearly two decades of strong performance, savings opportunities from direct-
install measures continue to diminish as efficient technologies become the market
standard. Customers increasingly seek more meaningful and flexible ways to save.
Savings opportunities from QHEC continue to diminish as efficient lighting becomes the
norm. Despite many years of success, the QHEC program must evolve to meet the
moment. Core elements of the QHEC experience, including easy enrollment, energy
education, and no-cost participation options, will remain available while providing stronger
connections to deeper energy efficiency and electrification retrofit opportunities.

**Introduction of a Multi-Modal Customer Pathway:** Customers will be able to participate
through a variety of channels, including digital intake, virtual assessments, and contractor
referrals. These pathways accommodate different customer readiness levels while
maintaining a progression toward deeper whole-home improvements.

[PDF p. 57 | printed p. 47]

**Expanded Use of Digital Tools and Automated Processes:** The program will
incorporate enhanced technology solutions to reduce manual processes, improve data
quality, support streamlined reporting, and strengthen quality assurance.

**Reduced Administrative Burden and Improved Cost Efficiency:** By eliminating duplicative
administrative activities across legacy programs, BGE expects to improve operational
efficiency, reduce overhead costs, and direct a greater share of program resources
toward customer services and incentives.

**Enhanced Customer Journey and Engagement Experience:** Customers will experience a
more intuitive and connected journey characterized by personalized recommendations,
fewer participation barriers, streamlined enrollment processes, and stronger connections
between initial engagement and deeper retrofit opportunities, including weatherization
and electrification projects completed over multiple phases.

#### 4.4.2 Goal Scenario Modifications

Because of the lower goal in the Low Scenario, BGE was able to prioritize investment in
comprehensive retrofit, weatherization, and electrification projects that deliver deeper
savings and improved cost-effectiveness, resulting in greater benefits per program dollar
spent. This resulted in the majority of the budget for the newly unified Home Upgrade
Program in the Low Scenario being dedicated to high value (dollar per GHG) measures.
In order to reach the higher goal of the High Scenario, BGE added significant budget for
the QHEC portion of the program which does not return nearly the same volume of GHG
emissions reductions per dollar spent as more comprehensive retrofit measures.
**Table 8: Scenario Comparison – Residential Home Upgrade**

| Residential Home Upgrade Scenarios | Total Budget | Total Lifecycle GHG |
| --- | --- | --- |
| High Scenario | $118,473,983 | 335,528 |
| Low Scenario | $88,509,149 | 271,325 |
| Difference | $29,964,834 | 64,202 |
| Difference (%) | 25% | 19% |

#### 4.4.3 Target Market

The Home Upgrade Program serves residential customers throughout BGE's service
territory and supports a broad range of energy efficiency, home performance, and
electrification improvements. The program is designed to accommodate varying building
types, ownership structures, customer objectives, and project scopes through flexible
participation pathways.

[PDF p. 58 | printed p. 48]

Eligible participants may include homeowners, renters, landlords, and property managers
of existing residential properties, including single-family detached homes, townhomes,
rowhouses, duplexes, condominiums, manufactured homes, and other qualifying
residential building types.

**Moderate Income Offering (Responses to Order 92522)**

BGE recognizes that some customers face affordability barriers but may not qualify for
traditional income-qualified programs. BGE worked with Stakeholders that included
DHCD and OPC when it sought to launch the Moderate Income Offer (MIO) in Cycle 6.
The launch of this offer was intentionally measured and controlled to minimize confusion
with offerings by DHCD, and also to ensure that customers were directed to the program
that was appropriate for them.

- We are seeking opportunities to increase participation among moderate-income
  households in Cycle 7 by onboarding additional in-network contractors for BGE
  and DHCD, as well as expanding program visibility through more integrated
  marketing with the larger Home Upgrade Program offerings. This includes
  integration with the website as well as other promotional materials.

- The MIO is not a subprogram, but an offering within the Home Upgrade Program.
  It is essentially an enhanced measure level incentives that are offered to qualified
  customers. BGE will work with OPC and other stakeholders on relevant MIO
  metrics to report in Cycle 7.

- BGE remains open to continued stakeholder discussions regarding expanded
  income thresholds, program design considerations, and coordination with DHCD-
  and MEA-administered offerings throughout the Cycle 7 period.

#### 4.4.4 Market Analysis and Trends

The residential housing stock throughout BGE's service territory presents significant
opportunities for energy efficiency and GHG reductions. Many homes were constructed
prior to adoption of modern energy codes and continue to experience elevated heating
and cooling loads due to insufficient insulation, envelope leakage, aging HVAC
equipment, and inefficient water-heating systems. These conditions create ongoing
opportunities for comprehensive retrofit activity.
The legacy QHEC and Home Performance offerings have provided customers with an
accessible pathway to energy-saving improvements for nearly two decades. While the
QHEC offering has been successful in connecting customers with no-cost energy-saving
opportunities, market transformation and evolving standards have reduced the savings
available from many traditional direct-install measures. As a result, deeper retrofit projects

[PDF p. 59 | printed p. 49]

increasingly represent the most effective opportunity to achieve meaningful GHG
reductions and long-term bill savings.
At the same time, recent program cycles have demonstrated growing customer and
contractor interest in comprehensive projects that bundle weatherization improvements
with equipment replacement and electrification measures. As illustrated in Figure 4 below,
BGE has observed increasing participation in Home Performance Program projects and
increasing average savings per project as customers pursue broader scopes of work and
take advantage of performance-based incentives.
**Figure 4: HPwES Program Annual Participation and Energy Savings Trends**

[DIAGRAM DESCRIPTION: Figure 4: HPwES Program Annual Participation and Energy Savings Trends]

The lifecycle MMBtu line sits around 200,000-330,000 in 2013-2020, rises to about 420,000 in 2021 and about 600,000 in 2024, and jumps to about 900,000 in 2025.

Despite these opportunities, upfront project costs remain a significant barrier to customer
participation. Many customers pursue efficiency improvements only when major
equipment fails or when comfort, health, durability, or utility bill concerns become
pronounced. Incentives, contractor support, education, and financial support therefore
remain critical tools for encouraging participation and helping customers pursue
comprehensive upgrades. Figure 5 illustrates the average home improvement project
costs and associated rebate claims for jobs completed through the HPwES Program
dating back to 2013 and including cost data from the first half of 2026.
Electrification represents one of the largest long-term opportunities for residential GHG
reductions. Customers replacing aging oil, propane, or natural gas heating systems with
high-efficiency heat pump technologies may achieve meaningful emissions reductions
while improving comfort and lowering operating costs. The Home Upgrade Program is
specifically designed to support these projects through a whole-home framework that
combines envelope improvements with efficient electric technologies.
BGE maintains an extensive network of participating contractors with expertise in home
performance, weatherization, HVAC installation, and electrification. The consolidated
program structure is expected to further strengthen contractor participation by simplifying
program requirements, reducing administrative burden, and creating clearer pathways
from customer assessment through project completion.

[PDF p. 61 | printed p. 51]

#### 4.4.5 Eligible Measures/Services

The program's whole-home design encourages customers to pursue bundled
improvements that maximize energy savings, improve comfort, and support broader
electrification objectives. While these bundled improvements generate the greatest
energy savings impacts, individual measure adoption will be allowed to meet customer
needs with ongoing engagement to encourage additional improvements over time.
Under the Home Upgrade Program’s new structure, customers will gain access to an
expanded array of measure options ranging from low-cost direct-install and low-cost
weatherization to full electrification upgrades. When possible, the program will offer pre-
enrollment of an eligible smart thermostat in BGE’s Connected Rewards program.
Eligible measures and services include:

- Energy audits
- Virtual home assessments
- Quick home energy check-ups
- Whole-house comprehensive energy audits
- Building envelope upgrades
- Air sealing improvements
- Attic insulation
- Wall insulation
- Floor and crawlspace insulation
- Rim joist insulation
- Distribution system upgrades
- Air duct sealing
- Air duct insulation
- HVAC distribution system improvements and modifications
- Heating and cooling equipment upgrades
- Air source heat pumps
- Cold-climate heat pumps
- Ground-source heat pumps
- Water heating equipment upgrades
- Unitary heat pump water heaters
- Split type heat pump water heaters
- Smart thermostats and controls
- HVAC tune-ups

[PDF p. 62 | printed p. 52]

- Mechanical ventilation improvements
- Combustion safety measures
- Other building performance improvements supported by program requirements
  and measures required by industry-recognized building performance standards

#### 4.4.6 Customer Incentives

See Attachment 2 for measures and incentives.
The Home Upgrade Program utilizes a combination of direct-install measures, audit
incentives, performance-based incentives, and targeted prescriptive-based incentive
packages to encourage participation in energy efficiency and electrification
improvements. Incentives are designed to reduce upfront costs, improve project
affordability, and encourage customers to pursue deeper whole-home energy upgrades.
Consistent with the program's whole-home approach, incentive levels are structured to
reward measures and projects that deliver meaningful energy savings, GHG reductions,
and customer benefits. Incentives may vary based on project scope, measure selection,
customer segment, and the level of verified savings achieved.
The program will continue offering performance-based incentives for comprehensive
retrofit projects that incorporate building envelope improvements, recognizing the critical
role these measures play in reducing energy consumption, improving comfort, and
supporting long-term building performance. Additional incentives may be available for
qualifying electrification measures and other targeted technologies that support program
objectives and evolving product options including an expanding market of highly
configurable efficient electric heat pump technologies for space, water heating, and
household appliances. Incentive levels will be periodically reviewed and adjusted, as
appropriate, to reflect changing market conditions, technology costs, customer adoption
trends, and ratepayer value.
Prescriptive-style incentive packages will continue to be offered for qualifying moderate-
income households to overcome first-cost barriers and increase access to comprehensive
energy efficiency and electrification upgrades. These enhanced incentives are designed
to bridge the gap between traditional market-rate offerings and income-qualified
programs, helping more customers benefit from deeper energy savings and bill
reductions.

[PDF p. 63 | printed p. 53]

#### 4.4.7 Marketing

The Home Upgrade Program marketing will focus on the awareness of its cohesive set
of energy solutions for residential customers, offering personalized home energy insights
and solutions through a network of participating contractors. Marketing strategies will
include educating and engaging residential customers to explore the Home Upgrade
Program while also providing marketing support to drive contractor-generated referrals.
After Home Upgrade visits, BGE may deploy follow-up marketing to encourage customers
to take additional energy efficiency steps or participate in other recommended programs.
For this market, the majority of Home Upgrade work occurs during periods of peak
seasonal discomfort and high energy bills. BGE will also place an emphasis on the
months leading to peak seasonal discomfort to maintain year-round program
participation. Thus, the strategic key time periods for marketing pushes are:

- Mid-January to mid-February: Customers experience late winter discomfort and
  begin early spring home improvement.

- Mid-May to late July: Customers experience peak summer heat discomfort and
  higher utility bills due to high energy use.

- October to November: Seasonal home improvement projects occur prior to the
  holidays.
  BGE will employ a variety of marketing tactics, including broadcast television, streaming
  audio and video, digital marketing, social media, content marketing, print magazine ads,
  direct mail, email, and utility-owned channels.

#### 4.4.8 Expected Savings, Costs, and Cost-Effectiveness

See Attachment 1 (ES Tables).

### 4.5 Residential New Construction Program

#### 4.5.1 Program Overview

BGE’s Residential New Construction Program encourages the construction of
high-performance homes that exceed applicable energy code requirements and deliver
long-term energy savings, customer affordability benefits, and GHG reductions. By
working directly with builders, developers, architects, engineers, and Home Energy
Rating System (HERS) professionals during the design and construction process, the
program helps incorporate energy-efficient construction practices when they can be
implemented most cost-effectively and with the least disruption to homeowners. The
program's core objective is to improve the efficiency and performance of new homes

## 7. Pilots

BGE is not proposing any new pilots for Cycle 7 at this time. The High Scenario maintains
a reduced budget, as compared to Cycle 6, for unidentified future projects to better
facilitate new measures or programs that may be necessary to achieve the higher goal
amount. If no pilots are developed, these funds will not be collected from customers. In
the Low Scenario, the budget is removed entirely with the understanding that
achievement will rely completely on the core set of programs.
**Table 18: Comparison of PIDD Budget in Cycle 6 and Cycle 7 Scenarios**

| PIDD Budget | Cycle Total |
| --- | --- |
| Cycle 6 | $9,466,542 |
| Cycle 7 - High | $6,000,000 |
| Cycle 7 - Low | $0 |

## 8. Programs Included in EmPOWER Energy and GHG Impacts With No Costs Recovered Via EmPOWER

### 8.1 Interconnected Solar Energy Generation

Under the Utility RELIEF Act, utilities may claim no more than 20% of the GHG emissions
reductions targets from solar energy-generating systems or solar energy-generating
facilities that are interconnected to the distribution system. BGE interprets this to include
both front-of-the-meter systems and behind-the-meter customer-sited systems with
interconnection agreements to the utility distribution system.

[PDF p. 118 | printed p. 108]

Given historical interconnection trends and the current interconnection queue, BGE
believes the 20% could be met in both goal scenarios presented and has forecasted 20%
achievement in each scenario. The Utility RELIEF Act also requires the PSC to approve
a framework for a new net energy metering (NEM) program to begin July 1, 2027. In turn,
the PSC published Notice Convening a Public Conference13 initiating Public Conference
78 (PC 78) as the proceeding for activities related to the NEM successor program. At this
time, it is not possible to predict the outcomes of PC 78 and how those outcomes may
impact solar energy generation interconnections during the 2027–2029 EmPOWER
program cycle.

### 8.2 Conservation Voltage Reduction

In Order No. 9095714, the PSC called for a working group to research and analyze the
appropriateness of counting utility CVR activities toward EmPOWER goals. On August 1,
2024, the CVR Working Group submitted its report to the PSC, which approved the
consensus recommendations that effectively end the impacts of CVR from counting
toward EmPOWER goals beyond 2026. Therefore, BGE is not forecasting CVR impacts
in the 2027–2029 EmPOWER plans.

### 8.3 Distribution Transformers

BGE has historically claimed savings from the installation of efficient distribution
transformers toward EmPOWER goals. While not explicitly acknowledged in the FPWG,
there is a possibility of conflation between the 20% cap on solar energy generation
systems from the Utility RELIEF Act and the existing legislative requirement that 80% of
the GHG emissions reductions “come from behind-the-meter programs.” While not all
solar energy generation that could be claimed according to the Utility RELIEF Act is
necessarily front of the meter, the inclusion of customer-owned or sited behind-the-meter
solar energy generation toward EmPOWER goals may not meet the definition of behind-
the-meter “programs.” As such, BGE does not plan to claim any savings from distribution
transformers toward the 2027–2029 EmPOWER goals.
13 Maryland Public Service Commission. ML 330109.
14 Maryland Public Service Commission. ML 306928.

[PDF p. 119 | printed p. 109]

## 9. EmPOWER Stakeholder Technical Conference Submissions and Recommendations

On January 23, 2026, PSC Staff hosted the Maryland EmPOWER Technical Conference
at BGE’s headquarters in Baltimore. The conference brought together utilities, regulators,
implementation partners, technology providers, and other stakeholders to discuss
emerging technologies and innovative concepts that may support Maryland's evolving
energy, affordability, reliability, and climate objectives. The event provided a collaborative
forum for participants to share perspectives, explore new approaches, and engage in
constructive dialogue regarding future opportunities within the EmPOWER framework.
BGE actively participated in these discussions, engaging with presenters and
stakeholders to better understand emerging technologies, market trends, and customer
needs. BGE asked questions to every presenter. Consistent with its long-standing
approach to EmPOWER, BGE remains committed to working collaboratively with
stakeholders to evaluate innovative solutions, improve program performance, and
advance customer affordability, system reliability, and environmental objectives.
Throughout the conference, presenters highlighted four innovative concepts for
consideration. ACEEE presented on thermal energy storage technologies and their
potential to provide customer and grid benefits through load shifting strategies. Renew
Home shared its Energy Shift Capacity VPP concept, which leverages connected devices
to provide flexible residential demand resources. ACEEE also presented an Energy Code
Compliance Support Program concept focused on improving building code compliance
through training, education, and technical assistance. In addition, HomeBoost introduced
its DIY Assessment concept designed to help customers identify energy-saving
opportunities and encourage participation in efficiency programs.
After evaluation, BGE determined that the proposed ACEEE thermal energy storage pilot
was not sufficiently aligned with existing residential program designs and delivery
mechanisms, and implementation would have required additional time, budget, and
program infrastructure beyond those currently in place.
In Cycle 6, as part of the directive of Order No. 92389, BGE implemented Renew Home’s
Energy Shift Capacity (“ESC”) concept using Unidentified PIDD Budgets. The capability
of the ESC events performed for both the Winter and Summer seasons will be evaluated
upon completion of the 2026 Summer season.

[PDF p. 120 | printed p. 110]

As described above in the Residential New Construction Program section above, BGE
will be establishing a Code Compliance Support initiative as proposed by ACEEE. This
begins with an evaluation exercise of establishing code compliance baseline conditions
in the territory and ultimately transitioning into training intervention to increase compliance
rates. Protocols for this type of activity are well documented across many jurisdictions
throughout the country and will be adapted for use in Maryland in close coordination with
the independent evaluators.
In Cycle 6, BGE also introduced HomeBoost’s DIY Assessment in its Home Performance
with ENERGY STAR program and continues to have discussions with HomeBoost on use
cases for the offering in Cycle 7.

| QA/QC Compliance Inspection | EE | $1,500 | per application |
|  | Electrification | $1,500 | per application |
| Audits | EE | $20,000 | per application |
| **Demand Response** |  |  |  |
| Load Control - C&I (Summer) | DR | $250 |  |
| Annual Enrollment/Loyalty Bonus Incentive - C&I (Summer) | DR | $250 |  |
| Load Control - C&I (Winter & Summer) | DR | $250 |  |
| Annual Enrollment/Loyalty Bonus Incentive - C&I (Winter & Summer) | DR | $250 |  |

1. Consistent with the Commission's Order 84569, not all utilities offer all products and services listed. ID: Order #84569 at 7.

2. Incentives are either paid at $.29/kWh, $125/MT CO2e up to 50 percent of the total project cost for retrofit projects, or up to 75 percent of the project cost for new construction or major renovation projects. Incentives are capped at up to $1

million per project.

3. Certain custom projects may produce both electric energy savings and fossil fuel-use reductions, resulting in corresponding greenhouse gas emission reductions. Under the proposed framework, incentives may be based on electric savings

($/kWh), GHG emission reductions ($/metric ton CO₂e avoided), or a combination thereof, allowing the Program to appropriately value projects that deliver benefits across multiple energy sources and end uses. The proposed methodology allows

incentives to reflect the combined electric energy savings and emissions benefits of a project rather than limiting eligibility to a single fuel or savings metric.

4. Tiered rebate amounts may be offered within the incentive ranges listed above for qualified measures that have varying characteristics (e.g. size, features, etc.)

5. Prescriptive rebates or performance based incentives may be offered in lieu of the performance or prescriptive amounts listed as long as they are within the 'up to' amounts listed.

6. Additional measure-specific incentive details may be outlined in an application's programs terms and conditions.

7. Virtual Commissioning (VCx) and Virtual Strategic Energy Management (VSEM) projects have a cap of 100% as they are Building Tune-Up services provided by a third-party vendor at no additional charge to the customer.
